They select investments (stocks, bonds, real estate, etc.) and aim to generate returns for their clients.\n* **Focus:** They focus on growing and preserving wealth for their clients through investment strategies.\n\n**Key Differences Summarized:**\n\n| Feature          | Group 1 (Banks)                               | Group 2 (Asset Managers)                     |\n|-----------------|-----------------------------------------------|----------------------------------------------|\n| Primary Role     | Financial intermediation, lending, investment banking | Investment management, wealth creation      |\n| Clients          | Individuals, businesses, governments          | Individuals, institutions, retirement plans |\n| Products/Services | Deposits, loans, checking accounts, investment banking services | Mutual funds, ETFs, hedge funds, investment advisory |\n| Revenue Model   | Interest income, fees for services           | Management fees based on assets under management |\n\n**Important Notes:**\n\n* **Overlap:** There can be some overlap between these categories. The first group, JP Morgan, Bank of America, Goldman Sachs, and Citi, are all investment banks. The second group, Blackrock, Fidelity, Franklin Templeton, and Invesco, are asset management firms. Here are some key differences between the two:\n\n1. **Business Model**: Investment banks primarily engage in activities such as underwriting, mergers and acquisitions, trading, and sales. They help companies and governments raise capital, manage financial risks, and provide advisory services. On the other hand, asset management firms manage investment funds for individuals, institutions, and governments. They make investment decisions on behalf of their clients, aiming to grow their clients' wealth over time.\n\n2. **Revenue Streams**: Investment banks generate revenue from fees for their services, such as underwriting fees, advisory fees, and trading commissions. Asset management firms, meanwhile, earn revenue primarily from management fees, which are a percentage of the assets they manage. They may also earn performance fees if their funds perform well.\n\n3. **Risk Profile**: Investment banks generally have a higher risk profile due to their involvement in trading and underwriting activities. They can be exposed to market risk, credit risk, and operational risk. Asset management firms, while not risk-free, typically have a lower risk profile as they are not directly involved in trading or underwriting. Their risk mainly comes from the performance of the assets they manage.\n\n4. **Regulation**: Both types of firms are heavily regulated, but the regulations differ. Investment banks are subject to regulations aimed at ensuring financial stability and preventing systemic risk, such as the Dodd-Frank Act in the U.S. Asset management firms are regulated to protect investors, with regulations focusing on issues like disclosure, transparency, and fiduciary duty.\n\n5. **Client Base**: Investment banks typically serve corporate and institutional clients, while asset management firms serve a broader range of clients, including individual investors, institutions, and governments.\n\nIn summary, while both groups of companies operate in the financial sector, they have different business models, revenue streams, risk profiles, regulatory environments, and client bases.",

This sparked further interest in understanding the ancient civilization and its relationship with the Roman Empire.\n\n4. **Archaeological Excavations**: The 19th century saw a surge in archaeological excavations in Tunisia, where Carthage was located.
